\begin{document}
\begin{titlepage}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
  We show that, for every fixed proper minor-closed graph class $\calG$ and every positive integer $n$ there exists a graph $U_n$ with $n\cdot\exp(O((\log n)^{3/4}))\le n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices and edges such that every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $U_n$.  

  This result generalizes and unifies the results of Dujmović, et al. (\textit{J. ACM} 2021) on induced-universal graphs and the results of Esperet, Joret, and Morin (\textit{J. London Math. Soc.} 2023) on subgraph-universal graphs from planar graphs to all proper minor-closed graph classes.
  It simultaneously improves the previous bounds for the number of vertices in induced-universal graphs (previously  $O(n^2)$ obtained as a corollary of a result of Devos et al. (\textit{J. Comb. Theory, Ser. {B}} 2004)) and a result of Gavoille and Labourel (\textit{ESA} 2007)) and  the number of edges in subgraph-universal graphs (previously  $O(n^{3/2})$ by Babai et al (\emph{Theory and Practice of Combinatorics} 1982)). 
  % For planar graphs, one of the subproblems we solve gives a small improvement to the result of Esperet et al, reducing the number of edges and vertices from $n\cdot\exp(O(\sqrt{\log n\log\log n}))$ to $n\cdot\exp(O(\sqrt{\log n}))$.
\end{abstract}
\end{titlepage}

\bigskip
\section{Introduction}

Let $\calG$ be a graph class (i.e., a set of graphs closed under isomorphism). A graph $U_n$ is \defin{$n$-universal} for $\calG$ if it ``contains'' every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$.  Here, the word ``contains'' can take on at least two meanings.  A graph $U_n$ is \defin{$n$-subgraph-universal} for $\calG$ if every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ is isomorphic to some subgraph of $U_n$.  A graph $U_n$ is \defin{$n$-induced-universal} for $\calG$ if every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ is isomorphic to some \emph{induced} subgraph of $U_n$.  These two notions of universality have largely been treated as separate concepts evaluated by different quality measures. A notable exception is an early work by \citet{chung:universal} that we discuss below.

Clearly, an $n$-subgraph-universal graph must have at least $n$ vertices, and the complete graph $K_n$ contains every $n$-vertex graph as a subgraph. Thus, for every $n\in\N$, $K_n$ is an $n$-vertex graph that is $n$-subgraph-universal for the set of all graphs. Consequently, research on subgraph-universal graphs has focused on minimizing the number of edges, especially when $\calG$ is a sparse class of graphs, such as trees, planar graphs, or $K_t$-minor-free graphs for some fixed $t$.  This line of research has a long and interesting history, dating back to the work of \citet{chung.graham.ea:on} in 1978.  Notable results include subgraph-universal graphs with $\Oh(n\log n)$ edges for trees and outerplanar graphs \cite{chung.graham.ea:on,chung:universal,kaul.kim.ea:universal}, $\Oh(n)$ edges for bounded-degree trees \cite{bhatt.chung.ea:universal}, $\Oh(kn \log (n/k+1))$ edges for graphs of treewidth at most $k$ \cite{kaul.kim.ea:universal}, and $n^{1+o(1)}$ edges for planar graphs \cite{EJM23}.

In the induced-universal setting, the vertex-disjoint union of all $n$-vertex graphs in $\calG$ is $n$-induced-universal, but this graph generally has an enormous number of vertices.\footnote{More precisely, let $\calG_n$ be the set of all $n$-vertex graphs in $\calG$. The universal graph is constructed by choosing one representative graph $G$ from each isomorphism class of $\calG_n$, and then taking the vertex-disjoint union of all such graphs.}  Thus, research on induced-universal graphs has focused on finding graphs with as few vertices as possible.  The subject of induced-universal graphs also has a long history, especially in computer science where it appears under the notion of adjacency labelling schemes (discussed below).  Notable results include induced-universal graphs with $\Oh(n)$ vertices for trees \cite{alstrup.dahlgaard.ea:optimal}, $n(\log(n/k))^{\Oh(k)}$ vertices for graphs of treewidth at most $k$ \cite{gavoille.labourel:shorter}, and a series of results leading to induced-universal graphs for planar graphs having $n\cdot\exp(\Oh(\sqrt{\log n}))$ vertices \cite{kannan.naor.ea:implicit,gavoille.labourel:shorter,chung:universal,spinrad:efficient,BGP22,dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency,gawrychowski.janczewski:simpler}.

A 1990 work by \citet{chung:universal} gave several theorems about induced-universal graphs that simultaneously bound both the number of vertices and the number of edges. This work showed the existence of $n$-induced-universal graphs for forests with $\Oh(n\log n)$ vertices and $\Oh(n^2)$ edges. Using the fact that the edges of a planar graph can be partitioned into three forests, she obtained $n$-induced-universal graphs for planar graphs with $\Oh((n\log n)^3)$ vertices and $\Oh(n^6)$ edges. For the next three decades, however, most research either focused on finding subgraph-universal graphs with few edges or on finding induced-universal graphs with few vertices.

% At the time (1990) the field was not very mature, so most of the results were far from optimal (e.g., $g_v(\calF_n)\in\Oh(n\log n)$ and $g_e(\calF_n)\in\Oh(n^2)$ for the class $\calF_n$ of $n$-vertex forests.)  Many of her results used the forest results in combination with arboricity (e.g., $g_v(\calP_n)\in\Oh((n\log n)^3)$ and $g_e(\calP_n)\in\Oh(n^6)$ for the class $\calP_n$ of $n$-vertex planar graphs.}

This dichotomy persisted until 2023, when \citet{EJM23} showed that, for the class of planar graphs, there exists an $n$-induced-universal graph with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices \emph{and edges}.  Thus, their result simultaneously satisfies the stronger requirement of being induced-universal while (nearly) optimizing both quality measures.  Their work is based on a construction of $n$-induced-universal graphs with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices for planar graphs due to \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency}.  In the current paper, we extend these results to all proper minor-closed graph classes.  
% In the special case of planar graphs we also give a (small) quantitative improvement on the number of edges and vertices compared to the results in \cin the constructions.

% In the current work, we extend this result by proving that, for every proper minor-closed class of graphs $\calG$, there exists an $n$-induced-universal graph with $n\cdot\exp((\log n)^{3/4})$ vertices and edges.  As part of our construction we also obtain an $n$-subgraph universal graph for planar graphs with $n \exp(O(\sqrt{\log n}))$ vertices and edges. 

\subsection{New Results}

As mentioned above, induced-universal graphs are closely related to adjacency labelling schemes.  The results in this paper and the techniques used to obtain them are best described using the language of adjacency labelling schemes, which we now define.
Let $\calG$ be a class of graphs and let $f:\N^+\to\R^+$ be a function.
We say that $\calG$ has an \defin{$f(n)$-bit adjacency labelling scheme} if there exists a function $A:(\set{0,1}^*)^2\to\set{0,1}$ (called an \defin{adjacency tester}) such that for all positive integers $n$, for every $n$-vertex graph $G\in\calG$, there exists a function $\ell: V(G)\to\set{0,1}^*$ such that
$|\ell(v)|\leq f(n)$ for each vertex $v$ in $G$, and such that
for every two vertices $u$, $v$ in $G$,
\[
  A(\ell(u),\ell(v))=\begin{cases}
    0 & \textrm{if $uv\notin E(G)$}, \\
    1 & \textrm{if $uv\in E(G)$}.
  \end{cases}
\]
(Such a function $\ell$ is called an \defin{adjacency labelling} of $G$ for $A$.) 

A graph $H$ is a \defin{minor} of a graph $G$ if a graph isomorphic to $H$ can
be obtained from a subgraph of $G$ by contracting edges. A class of graphs
$\calG$ is \defin{minor-closed} if every minor of every graph in
$\calG$ is also in $\calG$, and it is \defin{proper} if it is not the class of all graphs. Examples of proper minor-closed graph classes include planar graphs, graphs of treewidth at most $k$, graphs embeddable in a fixed
surface, linklessly embeddable graphs, knotlessly embeddable graphs, and, for every fixed graph $X$, the class of graphs with no $X$-minor. Note that every proper minor-closed class is contained in the class of $K_t$-minor-free graphs for some fixed $t$.  In this paper, we prove the following result. (All logarithms are in base $2$.)

\begin{thm}
  \label{main_result}
  Every proper minor-closed class of graphs has
  a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme.
\end{thm}

Note that all the dependence on the fixed proper minor-closed class in~\cref{main_result} is in the $o(\log n)$ term. Furthermore, \cref{main_result} is optimal up to the lower-order term; in our construction, this $o(\log n)$ term is $\Oh\left((\log n)^{3/4}\right)$. The proof of~\cref{main_result} is constructive: for every fixed proper minor-closed class $\calG$, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that takes a graph $G\in\calG$ as input and constructs the labelling $\ell:V(G)\to\{0,1\}^*$. We prove \cref{main_result} by introducing and studying a strengthening of adjacency labelling schemes called (weighted) mixed labelling schemes.

An adjacency labelling scheme for a class of graphs $\calG$ that uses an adjacency tester $A$ can be used to construct an $n$-induced-universal graph for $\calG$ having $\Oh(2^{f(n)})$ vertices, as follows:  We take $U_n$ to be the graph with vertex set $V(U_n):=\{0,1\}^{\le f(n)}$ and edge set $E(U_n):=\{\alpha\beta: \alpha, \beta \in V(U_n), \text{ and } A(\alpha,\beta)=1\}$.  For any $n$-vertex graph $G\in\calG$, there exists an adjacency labelling $\ell_G:V(G)\to\{0,1\}^{\le f(n)}$ of $G$ for $A$.  Then $U_n[\{\ell_G(v):v\in V(G)\}]$ is an induced subgraph of $U_n$ that is isomorphic to $G$.\footnote{A minor detail is that the labelling $\ell_G$ needs to be injective, for each $G\in\calG$.  This is the case for the adjacency labelling schemes developed in this paper. See~\cite[Section~2.1]{spinrad:efficient} for more details on the connection between adjacency labelling schemes and induced-universal graphs.} Thus, \cref{main_result} implies the existence of induced-universal graphs with a near-linear number of vertices for $n$-vertex graphs belonging to a fixed proper minor-closed class of graphs.

\begin{cor}\label{main_result_induced_universal}
  For every proper minor-closed graph class $\calG$, for every positive integer $n$,
  there exists a graph $U_n$ with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices such that every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $U_n$.
\end{cor}

Prior to the current work, the best result for adjacency labelling of $X$-minor-free graphs (even for $X=K_6$) required labels of length $(2+o(1))\log n$, leading to $n$-induced-universal graphs with $n^{2+o(1)}$ vertices.  (This result is obtained by combining a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for bounded-treewidth graphs due to \citet{gavoille.labourel:shorter} and a result of \citet{devos.ding.ea:excluding} establishing that the edges of every $X$-minor-free graph can be partitioned into two sets, each of which defines a graph of bounded treewidth.)  

The proof of \cref{main_result} makes use of a variant of the Robertson-Seymour Graph Minor Structure Theorem, which describes any graph in a proper minor-closed family in terms of a tree-decomposition.  To make this work, we resolve the problem of ``combining adjacency-labellings along tree-decompositions'' identified by \citet{BGP22} as the main difficulty in obtaining efficient adjacency-labelling schemes for proper minor-closed graph classes.  We do this by introducing and studying a generalization of adjacency labelling schemes called mixed labelling schemes. 

The adjacency labelling scheme we use to prove \cref{main_result} actually satisfies some additional conditions that not only imply \cref{main_result_induced_universal}, but also ensure that the universal graph $U_n$ is in fact sparse:
\begin{thm}\label{main_result_induced_universal_sparse}
    For every proper minor-closed graph class $\calG$, for every positive integer $n$,
    there exists a graph $U_n$ with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices and $n^{1+o(1)}$ edges such that every $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $U_n$.
\end{thm}

Prior to the current work, the best result for $n$-subgraph-universal graphs for $H$-minor-free graphs was a construction having $\Oh(n^{3/2})$ vertices and edges due to \citet{babai.chung.ea:on}, obtained using a (by now standard) application of $O(\sqrt{n})$ separators.  \Cref{main_result_induced_universal_sparse} improves this quantitatively by reducing the number of vertices and edges to $n^{1+o(1)}$ and qualitatively by constructing induced-universal graphs instead of subgraph-universal graphs.

We establish \cref{main_result_induced_universal_sparse} by introducing a notion of \emph{orientable} (mixed or adjacency) labelling schemes and showing that any orientable adjacency labelling scheme yields an induced-universal graph that is sparse.

\subsection{Relation to Recent Work}

The current paper is the full version of the conference paper \cite{dujmovic.gavoille.ea:adjacency_minor}, which only establishes \cref{main_result,main_result_induced_universal} using mixed labelling schemes.  In order to establish \cref{main_result_induced_universal_sparse}, we define orientable mixed labelling schemes.  The mixed labelling scheme in \cite{dujmovic.gavoille.ea:adjacency_minor} is not orientable, so the mixed labelling scheme presented here is significantly different. In \cite{dujmovic.gavoille.ea:adjacency_minor}, the tree indexing a tree-decomposition is partitioned into subtrees with at most $b$ vertices in each layer---a partition into \emph{$b$-skinny subtrees} whose weights decrease by a factor of $b$ between subtrees.  This then requires a solution for graphs having $b$-skinny tree-decompositions and a solution for graphs having tree-decompositions of height $\Oh(\log_b n)$ whose torsos themselves have $b$-skinny tree-decompositions. The mixed labelling scheme for graphs having $b$-skinny tree-decompositions in \cite{dujmovic.gavoille.ea:adjacency_minor} is not orientable, and there seems to be no easy way to fix this.

The current paper avoids this two-layer approach and thus avoids having to deal specifically with $b$-skinny tree-decompositions. Instead, the proof of \cref{lem:the_big_one} (the technical core of the current paper) performs a partition into subtrees each having $\Oh(b)$ nodes with the property that, within a single subtree, the nodes have weights that differ by a factor of at most $\Oh(b)$.  

Part of our proof requires that we devise an orientable mixed labelling scheme for a generalization of planar graphs. As in \cite{EJM23}, we do this by modifying the adjacency labelling scheme in \cite{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency}, which is based on the Planar Graph Product Structure Theorem  \cite{dujmovic.joret.ea:planar}.  The modifications we use are different from those in \cite{EJM23}, lead to a simplification of the original adjacency labelling scheme from \cite{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency}, and improve the $n^{o(1)}$ factor obtained in \cite{EJM23}.  Since graph product can be used to describe a variety of minor-closed and non-minor-closed graph classes we state our general result for graph products as a separate theorem.  Let $\calR_k$ denote the class of graphs $G$ for which there exists a graph $H$ of treewidth at most $k$ and a path $P$ such that $G$ is isomorphic to a subgraph of the strong graph product $H\boxtimes P$.

\begin{thm}\label{product_version}
  For all integers $k,n\ge 1$, there exists a graph $U^k_n$ with $n\cdot \exp(\Oh(k\sqrt{\log n}))$ vertices and edges such that every $n$-vertex graph in $\calR_k$ is isomorphic to an induced subgraph of $U^k_n$.
\end{thm}

The proof of \cref{product_version} combines the adjacency labelling scheme of \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} and its improvement by \citet{gawrychowski.janczewski:simpler} with logarithmic-height binary-tree decompositions discovered by several groups in the late 1990s and early 2000s in the context of parallel algorithms \cite{bodlaender.hagerup:parallel}, reachability queries \cite{chatterjee.ibsen-jensen.ea:optimal}, and space-efficient algorithms \cite{jain.tewari:space}. 

\paragraph{Organization of the Paper}

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows:  
\begin{itemize}
\item \cref{building_blocks} reviews some basic definitions and supporting results including treewidth, tree- and forest-decompositions, strong products, and prefix-free codes. This section also introduces the Graph Minor Product Structure Theorem (\cref{gmpst}) of \citet{dujmovic.joret.ea:planar} and then finishes by developing some tools for manipulating the tree-decompositions that come out of this theorem.
 \item \cref{weighted_mixed_section} begins by introducing weighted mixed labelling schemes and definitions of orientability for both adjacency labelling and mixed labelling schemes.  Mixed labelling schemes are a generalization of adjacency labelling schemes that also satisfy some additional requirements. In order to solve the problem of combining schemes along tree-decompositions, we need these schemes to support vertex-weighted graphs.  In order to obtain sparse induced-universal graphs, we also need these schemes to be orientable.  As a result of all these requirements, the complete definition of an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme is somewhat involved.  

After these definitions, this section proves a relatively easy result (\cref{add_apexes}) that applies to any graph family $\calG^{+a}$ whose members can be derived from a graph $G$ in a graph family $\calG$ via the addition of at most $a$ apex vertices.  Specifically, it shows that if $\calG$ admits an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme then $\calG^{+a}$ also admits an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme.  Although this result and its proof are not difficult, the proof is not particularly short; verifying that all aspects of the definition are satisfied takes some time.  This is a useful warm-up for what comes next.

Next is the main technical core of the paper, \cref{lem:the_big_one}, which establishes that the class $\calG'$ of graphs having tree-decompositions whose torsos come from a graph family $\calG$ that admits an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme has an efficient orientable adjacency labelling scheme. Any such class $\calG'$ therefore has $n$-induced-universal graphs with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices and edges.  

\item \cref{proof_main_result} combines the ingredients from the previous two sections with an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme for the class $\calR_k$ of product-structured graphs (\cref{gk_labelling_precise}) in order to prove the main results of the paper, \cref{main_result} and \cref{main_result_induced_universal_sparse}.  Briefly: for any proper minor-closed graph family $\calG$, \cref{gmpst} states that each graph in $\calG$ has a tree-decomposition whose torsos belong to $\calR_k^{+a}$, for some fixed $k$ and $a$.  By \cref{gk_labelling_precise,add_apexes}, $\calR_k^{+a}$ admits an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme.  By \cref{lem:the_big_one}, $\calG$ admits an efficient orientable adjacency labelling scheme.  The resulting adjacency labelling scheme implies \cref{main_result} and the fact that this scheme is orientable implies \cref{main_result_induced_universal_sparse}.

\item \cref{weighted_product_proof} establishes \cref{gk_labelling_precise}, the existence of an efficient orientable weighted mixed labelling scheme for $\calR_k$.  In addition to its application in proving \cref{main_result,main_result_induced_universal_sparse}, \cref{gk_labelling_precise} immediately implies \cref{product_version}. This result is left until the appendix because it requires a complete review and simplification of the adjacency labelling scheme in \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} and its improvement by \citet{gawrychowski.janczewski:simpler}.

% \item \Cref{conclusion} concludes with a summary and several open questions.
\end{itemize}

% \subsection{State of the Art}
% \label{ssec:state-of-art}

% Adjacency labelling schemes were introduced in the late 1980s by \citet{kannan.naor.ea:implicit} and independently in the PhD thesis of \citet{muller:local}. Since this initial work, labelling schemes have been developed for many other queries besides adjacency, including distances \citep{GAVOILLE200485}, ancestry and nearest common ancestors in
% trees \citep{AKM01,alstrup.rauhe:improved,DBLP:journals/mst/AlstrupGKR04}, reachability in planar
% digraphs \citep{DBLP:journals/jacm/Thorup04,DBLP:conf/focs/HolmRT15}, and flows and connectivity \citep{DBLP:journals/siamcomp/KatzKKP04}. A review of this vast literature is beyond the scope of this paper. Here we review results most relevant to the current work, focusing mainly on trees, bounded treewidth graphs, and planar graphs.

% Adjacency labelling schemes have also been studied for other general families of graph classes. At the dense end of the spectrum, \citet{DBLP:conf/stoc/BonamyEGS21} constructed
% asymptotically optimal adjacency labelling schemes for every hereditary class
% containing $2^{\Omega(n^2)}$ $n$-vertex graphs. In particular, they proved that comparability graphs admit a labelling with labels of length $n/4+o(n)$, which is best possible up to the lower-order term. Proper minor-closed classes lie at the opposite, sparse end of the spectrum, where the situation is markedly different, as we now discuss.

% There is a fascinating series of results improving on this simple scheme for

% adjacency labelling scheme for trees, which is optimal up to the $\Oh(1)$ term. Indeed, consider an $n$-vertex graph with no two vertices having the same neighborhood (e.g., a path for $n\geq4$). In any adjacency labelling scheme, each vertex must be assigned a distinct label and therefore one label must be of length at least  $\log (n+1)-1$.

Thus, all labels are of length $(2+o(1))\log n$.

% Planar graphs are a prominent example of a proper minor-closed class of graphs and there is a long history of research

% Since planar graphs are $5$-degenerate,
% they admit a simple $\lceil6\log n\rceil$-bit adjacency labelling scheme, which was already observed by \citet{muller:local}.
% \citet{kannan.naor.ea:implicit} use a similar approach that makes use of the fact that planar graphs have arboricity~3 (so their edges can be partitioned into three forests \citep{nash-williams:edge-disjoint}) to devise an adjacency labelling scheme for planar graphs whose labels have length at most $\lceil4\log n\rceil$. This length was reduced to $3\log{n} + \Oh(\log^*{n})$ by the improved scheme of \citet{AR02} for arboricity-$k$ graphs. 
% Of course, the later result of \citet{gavoille.labourel:shorter} also applies to planar graphs, and gives a scheme with labels of length $(2+o(1))\log n$.
% The most recent breakthrough came with the application of the product structure theorem for planar graphs by \citet{dujmovic.joret.ea:planar}.
% In 2020, \citet{BGP22} recognized that the product structure is particularly useful for adjacency labelling, breaking the $(2+o(1))\log n$  barrier by introducing a scheme with labels of length $(\nicefrac{4}{3}+o(1))\log n$.
% Finally, in 2020 \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} used the product structure theorem to give a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for planar graphs, which is optimal up to the lower order term. In \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency}, the lower order term is in $\Oh(\sqrt{\log n\log\log n})$.  \citet{gawrychowski.janczewski:simpler} later introduced a simplification that reduces the lower order term to  $\Oh(\sqrt{\log n})$ and allows for the adjacency testing function to be implemented in constant time in a realistic model of computation.

% The adjacency labelling scheme of \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} works for  graphs embeddable in any fixed surface, and more generally for any minor-closed class excluding a fixed apex graph as a minor. Here a graph $X$ is \defin{apex} if $X$ can be made planar by the removal of at most one vertex. Since $K_5$ is apex, this implies a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for $K_5$-minor-free graphs. Such apex-minor-free classes are the limit of this approach, since a minor-closed class $\calG$ has `product structure' if and only if some apex graph is not in $\calG$. Indeed, the existence of a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for $K_t$-minor-free graphs is stated as an open problem by \citet[Section~6, Problem~2]{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency}. \citet{BGP22} likewise anticipated that such labelling schemes might extend to all proper minor-closed classes via the graph minor structure theorem of Robertson and Seymour, and identified the difficulty: ``we do not know how to combine labeling schemes along tree decompositions''.
% %\citet{BGP22} anticipated that the existence of $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling schemes might extend to all proper minor-closed classes via the graph minor structure theorem of Robertson and Seymour, and identified where the difficulty lies: ``combining the labelings along a tree decomposition into nearly embeddable parts seems to be the main issue''. 
%  Prior to the present work, the best result for $K_t$-minor-free graphs, $t > 5$, was the $(2+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme of \citet{gavoille.labourel:shorter}, who also conjectured that labels of length $\log n + \Oh(1)$ suffice for
% every proper minor-closed class of graphs.



% Finally we discuss the relationship between our result and the Implicit Graph Conjecture. A hereditary graph class is \defin{small} if it has $2^{O(n\log n)}$ labelled $n$-vertex graphs for all $n$. For example, every proper minor-closed graph class is small~\citep{NSTW06}. The Implicit Graph Conjecture~\citep{kannan.naor.ea:implicit} asserted that every small graph class has an $O(\log n)$-bit adjacency labelling scheme. Proper minor-closed classes satisfy this conjecture, and indeed they have labels of only $(1+o(1))\log n$ bits by \cref{main_result}. The conjecture in general was disproved by \citet{HH22}, who in fact showed there exist small graph classes for which every adjacency labelling scheme needs $\Omega(n^{1/2-\epsilon})$ bits. See \citep{Bonnet-etal-ICALP24,Bonnet-etal-SJC24,Alon24} for recent extensions. In light of the counterexamples of \cite{HH22}, it remains an interesting problem to determine which hereditary classes have $O(\log n)$-bit, or even $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit, adjacency labelling schemes. Our result shows that excluding a fixed minor suffices for the strongest conclusion.

% \label{KeyTools}

% In this paper, all graphs are finite, simple, and undirected.  For a graph $G$, \defin{$V(G)$} denotes the vertex-set of $G$, \defin{$E(G)$} denotes the edge-set of $G$.  A \defin{clique} in a graph is a non-empty set of pairwise adjacent vertices.   Let $G$ be a graph and $X\subseteq V(G)$. By $G[X]$ we denote the subgraph of $G$ induced on $X$. For other standard graph theory terms, we refer the reader to the textbook by \citet{diestel:graph}.

% A \defin{tree-decomposition} of a graph $G$ is a pair $(T,(B_x \mid x \in V(T)))$, where $T$ is a tree and $B_x \subseteq V(G)$ for every $x \in V(T)$, with the following properties:
% \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\alph*)]
%   \item for each vertex $u$ in $G$, the subgraph of $T$ induced by $\{x \in V(T) \mid u \in B_x\}$ is non-empty and connected; and
%   \item for each edge $uv$ in $G$, there exists $x\in V(T)$ such that $u,v\in B_x$.
% \end{enumerate*}
% We call the sets $B_x$ the \defin{bags} of the tree-decomposition and, for each edge $xy$ of $T$, $B_x\cap B_y$ is an \defin{adhesion} of the tree-decomposition.  When $T$ is rooted and $x$ is the parent of $y$, $B_x\cap B_y$ is the \defin{parent adhesion} of $y$. The \defin{parent adhesion} of the root of $T$ is the empty set. The \defin{home} of a vertex $v$ of $G$ is the unique $x\in V(T)$ such that $v\in B_x$ and $v$ is not in the parent-adhesion of $x$. The \defin{adhesion-width} of a tree-decomposition is the maximum size of an adhesion. For a tree-decomposition $\calT:=(T,(B_x \mid x\in V(T)))$ of a graph $G$, for each  $x\in V(T)$, the \defin{torso} of $x$, denoted by \defin{$\torso{G}{\calT,B_x}$}, is the graph with vertex-set $B_x$ in which two distinct vertices $u$ and $v$ are adjacent if $uv \in E(G)$, or there exists $y \in N_T(x)$  such that $u,v \in B_x \cap B_y$.


% We now have enough background to sketch the proof of our main result.  We begin by describing natural approaches to the problem, where these approaches have issues, and then explain how we overcome these issues. By the above-mentioned results, it suffices to consider a graph class $\calG$ with the following property:
% For every $G\in\calG$, there exists a tree-decomposition $\calT:=(T,(B_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ of $G$ such that each torso of $\calT$ comes from a monotone graph class $\calG'$ that has a $((1+o(1))\log n)$-bit adjacency labelling scheme.  Let $A'$ be the adjacency tester for the labelling scheme on $\calG'$.

This is because later we reuse $A_x$ for other meaning, parent in tree-dec, whereas "tester" algorithms and predicate are rarely used, and of very different nature.}

% Let $G$ be an $n$-vertex graph in $\calG$ and let $\calT:=(T,(B_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ be a tree-decomposition of $G$ such that each torso of $\calT$ is in the class $\calG'$.  Each edge of $G$ is present in $G[B_x]$ for at least one $x\in V(T)$. Thus, a natural first approach is to compute an adjacency labelling of $G[B_x]$ for $A'$, for each $x\in V(T)$.  The problem with this approach is that a vertex $v$ of $G$ can appear in many bags of $\calT$, so it can have many labels. Simply concatenating these labels would result in labels of length much greater than $\log n$.

% \paragraph{Assigning homes:} For each $x\in V(T)$, let $A_x$ be the parent adhesion of $x$ in $\calT$ and let $B^-_x:=B_x\setminus A_x$.  (Thus $x$ is the home of each $v\in B^-_x$.) Then $\{B^-_x\mid x\in V(T)\}$ is a partition of $V(G)$.  A natural second attempt is to compute an adjacency labelling $\ell_x$ of $G[B^-_x]$ for $A'$, for each $x\in V(T)$.  This leaves two closely-related issues:
% \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep]
%   \item For two vertices $v\in B^-_x$ and $w\in B^-_y$ with $x\neq y$, the adjacency test $A'(\ell_x(v),\ell_y(w))$ does not produce a useful result, since $\ell_x$ and $\ell_y$ are labellings of different graphs. To avoid misusing $A'$ this way, we need a way of determining that $x\neq y$ from the labels of $v$ and $w$.
%   \item In the case where $x\neq y$, we need an alternative way of testing if $v$ and $w$ are adjacent, since $vw$ is not present in $\bigcup_{x\in V(T)}G[B^-_x]$ even when $vw\in E(G)$.
% \end{enumerate}

% These two issues make precise the difficulty that was also identified by \citet{BGP22}, namely
% that of combining labellings along a tree-decomposition. The tools we develop
% next are designed to overcome it.


% \paragraph{2-Part labels:}
% To deal with the first issue, we can assign each vertex $v$ a 2-part label $\langle x(v),\ell_x(v) \rangle$, where $x(v)$ is a unique identifier for the node $x$ of $T$ such that $v\in B^-_x$.  Doing this naïvely could easily lead to a label length of $2\log n + o(\log n)$.  This can be avoided by using a variable-length code so that $|x(v)|=\log n-\log|B^-_{x(v)}|+\Oh(1)$.  This way, $|x(v)|+|\ell_x(v)|=|x(v)|+\log|B^-_{x(v)}|+o(\log n)=\log n + o(\log n)$.  This solves the first of the two problems above, but still leaves us with the second problem.  There is still no way of testing adjacency between vertices with different homes.

% \paragraph{Mixed labelling schemes:} An important contribution of the current work is to introduce a generalization of adjacency labelling schemes, called \defin{weighted mixed labelling schemes}, that attempts to solve both of these problems simultaneously. The weights play an important but technical role. For the simplicity of this overview we omit the role of the weights for now, speaking only of \emph{mixed labelling schemes}, and return to it at the end of the overview when stating our main technical result. The formal definition appears at the beginning of \cref{weighted_mixed_section}. Informally, a mixed labelling $\mu$ is defined for a pair of graphs $Q^+$ and $Q$, where $Q$ is a spanning subgraph of $Q^+$, and $\mu$ defines labels for both vertices and cliques of $Q^+$. The vertex labels assigned by $\mu$ are used with an adjacency tester $A$ for the spanning subgraph $Q$.  That is, for any two vertices $v$ and $w$ of $Q$, $A(\mu(v),\mu(w)) = 1$ if and only if $vw\in E(Q)$.  In addition to the vertex and clique labels, a mixed labelling assigns, for each clique $K$ of $Q^+$ and each vertex $u\in K$, a short \defin{local identifier} $\kappa(K,u)$ for $u$ in $K$. These clique, vertex, and local identifiers are used with an \defin{identity tester} $I$.  For any clique $K$ of $Q^+$, any vertex $u\in K$, and any vertex $v$ of $Q$, $I(\mu(K),\kappa(K,u),\mu(v))=1$ if and only if $v=u$.

% Loosely speaking, a class has an \emph{efficient} mixed labelling scheme if it has a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit mixed labelling scheme. This notion of efficiency is defined formally in the introduction to \cref{weighted_mixed_section}. \Cref{weighted_product_proof} shows that the adjacency labelling scheme for graphs in $\calR_k$ given by \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} and its improvements by \citet{gawrychowski.janczewski:simpler} can be extended to give an efficient mixed labelling scheme for $\calR_k$.  \Cref{add_apexes} shows that the addition of apex vertices to obtain the class $\calR^{+a}_k$ can be accommodated by any mixed labelling scheme.  The resulting mixed labelling scheme has clique and vertex labels of length $\log n + \Oh(\sqrt{\log n})$ (see \cref{gk_labelling_precise}).

% Thus, for every proper minor-closed graph family $\calG$, every graph $G$ in $\calG$ has a tree-decomposition $\calT$ of bounded adhesion-width whose torsos come from a class that has an efficient mixed labelling scheme.  This is the only property of proper minor-closed graph classes that we use.

By treating each subtree as a single giant bag, this gives a tree-decomposition $\calT'$ of $G$ whose height is at most $\log_b n$, and in which each torso of $\calT'$  has a $b$-skinny tree-decomposition whose bags are bags of $\calT$. Note that at this point we do not yet know how to label the (typically large) bags of $\calT'$ themselves. That is the purpose of the next step.

We then reuse ideas of \citet{gavoille.labourel:shorter} and \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} to handle adjacency testing between vertices in different homes and extend these to provide the remaining functionality (clique labels, local identifiers, and identity testing) required for a mixed labelling scheme.  The resulting mixed labelling scheme has labels of length $\log n + O(\log b) + o(\log n)$.

% Without much extra work, the adjacency-labelling solution for short tree-decompositions can be extended to provide an efficient mixed labelling scheme.  Since this only relies on the underlying tree-decomposition having bounded adhesion-width and having torsos in a class $\calG$ that has an efficient mixed labelling scheme, our main result is thus described concisely by the following theorem:

% %We can now say what the weights are for. In a \emph{weighted} mixed labelling scheme, each vertex $v$ carries a positive weight $\omega(v)$ and vertex labels must satisfy the Kraft-type bound $|\mu(v)|\le\log\omega(G)-\log\omega(v)+o(\log n)$; heavier vertices thus receive shorter labels. When recursing on a tree-decomposition, we inflate the weight of each vertex according to the total weight hanging below it, which makes label lengths telescope along root-to-leaf paths. With this notion (defined formally in \cref{weighted_mixed_section}), our main technical result reads:


% \begin{thm}\label{PlainRealMainTechnical}
%   Let $\calG$ be a hereditary graph class that has an efficient weighted mixed labelling scheme. Then, for any fixed integer $k\geq 0$, the class of graphs that have a tree-decomposition of adhesion-width at most $k$ whose torsos belong to $\calG$ also has an efficient weighted mixed labelling scheme.
% \end{thm}

% We can now say what the weights are for. For the recursion behind this theorem, plain mixed labelling schemes are not enough. We need a refinement in which each vertex $v$ carries a positive weight $\omega(v)$ and vertex labels satisfy the Kraft-type bound $|\mu(v)|\le\log\omega(V(G))-\log\omega(v)+o(\log n)$. Intuitively, weights let heavier vertices receive shorter labels; when recursing on a tree-decomposition, inflating the weight of a vertex according to the total weight hanging below it makes the label lengths telescope along root-to-leaf paths. This notion of a \defin{weighted mixed labelling scheme} (and the accompanying notion of \emph{efficiency}) is defined formally in \cref{weighted_mixed_section}.


% A quantitative version of \cref{PlainRealMainTechnical} appears as \cref{lem:the_big_one} in \cref{working_with_tree_decomps}.

% \subsection{Paper Organization}

% The remainder of the paper is organized as follows:  

% \Cref{building_blocks} reviews some basic background material and shows how to
% partition any tree-decomposition into $b$-skinny subtrees so that contracting
% each subtree yields a tree-decomposition of height at most $\log_b n$, as outlined above.    \Cref{weighted_mixed_section} formally defines weighted mixed labelling schemes and proves \cref{PlainRealMainTechnical}.
% We conclude in \Cref{conclusion} by explaining how the constructive nature of \cref{main_result} leads to a
% polynomial-time labelling algorithm.

\section{Building blocks}\label{building_blocks}

Let \defin{$\N$} be the set of all nonnegative integers, and let \defin{$\N^+$} be the set of all positive integers.  For $p\in\N^+$, let $\mathdefin{[p]} := \set{1,\dots,p}$. 

In this paper, each graph $G$ we consider is a simple graph with vertex set denoted by $V(G)$ and edge set denoted by $E(G)$. For an edge of $G$ with endpoints $v$ and $w$, we use the shorthand notation $vw$ instead of $\{v,w\}$ for the edge, with occasional exceptions.  For a graph $G$ and a vertex $v$ of $G$, $\mathdefin{N_G(v)}:=\{w\in V(G):vw\in E(G)\}$ is the \defin{(open) neighbourhood} of $v$ in $G$.  For any set $S$, \defin{$G[S]$} is the graph with vertex set $\mathdefin{V(G[S])}:=S\cap V(G)$ and edge set $\mathdefin{E(G[S])}:=\{vw\in E(G):v,w\in S\}$, which we call the subgraph of $G$ \defin{induced} by $V(G)\cap S$.  For any set $S$, $G-S:=G[V(G)\setminus S]$. For any $R\subseteq E(G)$, $G-R$ is the graph with vertex set $V(G)$ and edge set $E(G)\setminus R$. A non-empty subset $K$ of vertices of $G$ is a \defin{clique} in $G$ if $vw\in E(G)$ for each $\{v,w\}\in\binom{K}{2}$.  

A \defin{graph class} is a set of graphs closed under isomorphism. A graph class $\calG$ is \defin{hereditary} if, for every $G\in\calG$, every induced subgraph of $G$ is also in $\calG$. For a graph class $\calG$ and a nonnegative integer $a$, define \defin{$\calG^{+a}$} as the class of graphs $G$ such that $G-X\in\calG$ for some $X\subseteq V(G)$ with $|X|\leq a$.

\subsection{Trees and Forests}

For a rooted tree $T$ with root $r\in V(T)$ and a node $x$ of $T$, define \defin{$P_T(x)$} to be the path in $T$ from $x$ to $r$.  A \defin{forest} $F$ is a graph (possibly disconnected) each of whose components is a tree.  If each component of a forest $F$ is a rooted tree, then $F$ is a \defin{rooted} forest. For a node $x$ in a rooted forest $F$, let $\mathdefin{P_F(x)}:=P_T(x)$, where $T$ is the component of $F$ that contains $x$.

Let $F$ be a rooted forest.  For each $x\in V(F)$, the \defin{$F$-depth} of $x$, denoted \defin{$\depth_F(x)$}, is the number of edges in $P_F(x)$. The \defin{height} of $F$, denoted $\mathdefin{\height(F)}:=\max_{x\in V(F)}\depth_F(x)$, is the maximum $F$-depth of a node in $F$. For each $y\in V(F)$ and each $x\in V(P_F(y))$ (including $y$), the node $x$ is an \defin{$F$-ancestor} of $y$ and $y$ is an \defin{$F$-descendant} of $x$.  For each $x\in V(F)$, \defin{$F_x$} denotes the subtree of $F$ induced by all $F$-descendants of $x$. 
For each edge $xy$ of $F$, where $x$ is an $F$-ancestor of $y$, we say that $x$ is the \defin{$F$-parent} of $y$ and $y$ is an \defin{$F$-child} of $x$. 
% We say that a set $S\subseteq V(F)$ is an \defin{$F$-antichain}, if there are no distinct $x,y\in S$ such that $x$ is a $F$-ancestor of $y$.  
(When there is no danger of ambiguity, we may drop the leading $F$- from $F$-depth, $F$-ancestor, $F$-descendant, $F$-parent, and $F$-child.) We treat each subgraph $F'\subseteq F$ as a rooted forest in which each component $T$ of $F'$ is rooted at the node of $T$ having minimum $F$-depth.  
% For each $i\in\N^+$, the \defin{$i$-th layer} of $F$ is $\mathdefin{L_i(F)}:=\{x\in V(F):\depth_F(x)=i-1\}$. Thus, $L_1(F)$ is the set of roots of components of $F$.

For a rooted tree $T$ and any non-empty set $S\subseteq V(T)$, the \defin{lowest common $T$-ancestor} of $S$, denoted \defin{$\lca_T(S)$}, is the node in $\bigcap_{x\in S} V(P_T(x))$ of maximum $T$-depth.

\subsection{Forest-Decompositions and Tree-Decompositions}

It will be convenient to work briefly with forest-decompositions, which are the natural generalization of tree-decompositions, allowing the graph indexing the bags to be a forest.  A \defin{forest-decomposition} of a graph $G$ is a pair $\calF:=(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F)))$, where $F$ is a forest and $(B_x\mid x\in V(F))$ is a collection of subsets of $V(G)$ indexed by the nodes of $F$ such that  
\begin{enumerate*}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item for each edge $vw$ of $G$, there exists $x\in V(F)$ with $v,w\in B_x$; and
  \item for each vertex $v$ of $G$, $F[\{x\in V(F)\mid v\in B_x\}]$ is non-empty and connected.
\end{enumerate*}  
The sets $B_x$ are called the \defin{bags} of $\calF$. When $F$ is a tree, $\calF$ is a \defin{tree-decomposition}. The \defin{width} of a tree-decomposition $(T,(B_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ is $\max_{x\in V(T)}|B_x|-1$. The \defin{treewidth} of a graph $G$, denoted $\mathdefin{\tw(G)}$, is the minimum width of a tree-decomposition of $G$. 

A forest-decomposition is \defin{rooted} if it is indexed by a rooted forest. For each edge $xy$ of $F$, $B_x\cap B_y$ is called an \defin{adhesion} of $\calF$, and we say that this adhesion is incident to $x$ and $y$.  For each $x\in V(F)$, the graph \defin{$\torso{G}{\calF,B_x}$} obtained from $G[B_x]$ by adding edges so that each adhesion incident to $x$ becomes a clique is called the \defin{torso} of $\calF$ at $x$. For a root node $x\in V(F)$, the \defin{parent adhesion} of $x$ is the empty set. If a non-root node $y\in V(F)$ has parent $x$, the \defin{parent adhesion} of $y$ is $B_x\cap B_y$. When $\calF$ is rooted, the \defin{home} (or \defin{home node}) of a vertex $v\in V(G)$ is the unique node $x\in V(F)$ such that $v\in B_x$ and $v$ is not in the parent adhesion of $x$.  The \defin{adhesion-width} of $\calF$ is the size of its largest adhesion (or zero if $\calF$ has no edges).





% We make use of the fact that rooted tidy forest-decompositions remain tidy when we remove the root bags and their contents.  More precisely, let $\calF:=(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F)))$ be a rooted tidy forest-decomposition of a graph $G$, let $R$ be the set of roots in $F$, let $B_R:=\bigcup_{r\in R}B_r$, and let $C_x:=B_x\setminus B_R$ for each $x\in V(F)$.  Then $(F-R,(C_x\mid x\in V(F-R)))$ is a rooted tidy rooted forest-decomposition of $G-B_R$.

\subsection{Graph Product Structure}

The \defin{strong product} of graphs~$A$ and~$B$, denoted by~${A \boxtimes B}$, is the graph with vertex set ${V(A) \times V(B)}$, where distinct vertices ${(v,x),(w,y) \in V(A) \times V(B)}$ are adjacent if
${v=w}$ and ${xy \in E(B)}$, or
${x=y}$ and ${vw \in E(A)}$, or
${vw \in E(A)}$ and~${xy \in E(B)}$.
For an integer $k\geq 0$, let \defin{$\calR_{k}$} be the class of graphs isomorphic to a subgraph of $H\boxtimes P$ for some graph $H$ of treewidth at most $k$ and some path $P$.  
Our starting point is the following variant of the Graph Minor Structure Theorem:
% The main result of \citet{dujmovic.esperet.ea:adjacency} is a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for the class $\calR_k$, for any fixed $k$. \cref{gk_labelling_precise} extends this result to show that $\calR_k$ has an efficient weighted mixed labelling scheme.

% For any graph class $\calG$, let \defin{$\calG^{+a}$} be the class of graphs $G$ such that $G-X$ is in $\calG$ for some $X\subseteq V(G)$ with $|X|\leq a$.  
% It is a simple exercise to show that any $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for a graph class $\calG$ implies the existence of a $(1+o(1))\log n$-bit adjacency labelling scheme for $\calG^{+a}$.  \Cref{add_apexes} shows that the same is true for efficient weighted mixed adjacency labelling schemes. 


\begin{thm}[\protect Graph Minor Product Structure Theorem~\citep{dujmovic.joret.ea:planar}]
  \label{gmpst}
  For every proper minor-closed class $\calG$, there exist integers $k,a\geq 0$ such that every graph in $\calG$ has a tree-decomposition in which every torso is in $\calR_k^{+a}$.
\end{thm}





\subsection{Weight Functions}

For a non-empty set $S$, a \defin{weight function} over $S$ is a function $\omega:S\to\R^+$. For $X\subseteq S$, we use the shorthand $\mathdefin{\omega(X)}:=\sum_{x\in X}\omega(x)$.  When $S:=V(G)$ is the vertex-set of a graph $G$ and $H$ is a subgraph of $G$, we use the shorthand $\mathdefin{\omega(H)}:=\omega(V(H))$.

Let $\omega:S\to\R^+$ be a weight function.  For each $x\in S$, the \defin{(Kraft) ideal codeword length} for $x$ is $\log\omega(S)-\log\omega(x)$.
It will be convenient to place some upper bound on the Kraft ideal codeword length or, equivalently, on the ratio of total weight $\omega(S)$ to minimum weight $\min_{x\in S}\omega(x)$.  A weight function $\omega':S\to \N^+$ is \defin{nice} if for every $x\in S$,
\[
  \log \omega'(S) - \log \omega'(x) \leq \log |S| + 2\enspace.
\]
(Note that nice weight functions always assign positive integer weights.)

\begin{obs}\label{obs:nice}
  For every weight function $\omega:S\to\R^+$, there exists a nice weight function $\omega':S\to\N^+$ such that $\log\omega'(S)-\log\omega'(x)\le\min\{\log|S|,\;\log\omega(S)-\log\omega(x)\}+2$, for each $x\in S$.
\end{obs}

\begin{proof}
  By dividing every weight by $\min_{x\in S}\omega(x)$, we may assume, without loss of generality, that $\omega(x)\ge 1$ for each $x\in S$, since this does not change the value of the right-hand side in the inequality.
  For each $x\in S$, define $\omega'(x):=\ceil{\max\{\omega(S)/|S|,\; \omega(x)\}}$.  Then $\omega'(S)<\sum_{x\in S}(\omega(S)/|S|+\omega(x)+1)= 2\omega(S)+|S|\le 3\omega(S)$.  For each $x\in S$, the inequality $\omega'(x)\ge \omega(x)$ implies that $\log\omega'(S)-\log\omega'(x)\le\log \omega(S)-\log\omega(x)+\log 3$ and the inequality $\omega'(x)\ge \omega(S)/|S|$ implies that $\log\omega'(S)-\log\omega'(x)\le \log\omega(S)-\log(\omega(S)/|S|) + \log 3 = \log|S| + \log 3$.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Shannon and Shannon--Fano--Elias Codes}

A rooted tree $T$ is a \defin{binary tree} if each node of $T$ has at most two children among which at most one is the \defin{left child} and at most one is the \defin{right child}.
A binary tree $T$ is \defin{full} if for every node $x$ of $T$,
either $x$ has two children or $x$ has no children.
A full binary tree $T$ is \defin{complete} if all the leaves of $T$ have the same depth in $T$.
Let $T$ be a binary tree. There is a natural ordering, called \defin{left-to-right ordering}, of the leaves of $T$, such that for every node $x$, if $x$ has two children, then all the leaf descendants of the left child of $x$ precede all the leaf descendants of the right child of $x$.

% The following result is a variant of Alphabetic Binary Trees. Similar results (with the constant $3$ replaced by $2$) are well-known and there are several proofs \cite{knuth:art_iii,mehlhorn:nearly,gilbert.moore:variable_length}. We provide a proof of the weaker statement here because it illustrates a simple but powerful technique---\defin{simulating weight by multiplicity}---that we will use again later.

\begin{lem}\label{shannon_fano_elias_tree}
  Let $S$ be a linearly ordered set and let $\omega:S\to\R^+$. Then there exists a binary tree $T$ such that $S$ is the set of leaves of $T$,
  the linear order on $S$ agrees with the left-to-right-order of the leaves in $T$, and for each $x\in S$
  \[
    \depth_{T}(x)\le \log\omega(S) - \log\omega(x) + \Oh(1) \enspace.
  \]
\end{lem}

A \defin{code} for a set $S$ is an injective function $\rho:S\to\{0,1\}^*$. A code $\rho:S\to\{0,1\}^*$ is \defin{prefix-free} if for all distinct $x,y\in S$, $\rho(x)$ is not a prefix of $\rho(y)$.
In coding theory, \cref{shannon_fano_elias_tree} is the basis of the Shannon--Fano--Elias coding scheme, as we now explain.  By assigning each edge $e$ of a binary tree $T$ a $0$ or $1$ depending on whether $e$ joins a parent to its left or right child, we obtain an encoding of each root-to-leaf path in $T$ as a binary string, which gives a prefix-free code for the leaves of $T$. Doing this with the tree from \cref{shannon_fano_elias_tree}, we obtain the following.

\begin{cor}\label{shannon_fano_elias_code}
  There exists a function $F:(\{0,1\}^*)^2\to\{0,1\}^*$ such that, for every $n\in\N^+$, every set $S:=\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$, and every weight function $\omega:S\to\R^+$, there exists a prefix-free code $\rho:S\to \{0,1\}^*$ for $S$ and functions $\overleftarrow{\pi}:S\to\{0,1\}^*$, $\overrightarrow{\pi}:S\to\{0,1\}^*$ such that:
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=\rm(\alph*)]
    \item\label{sfec_len} $|\rho(x_i)|\le \log\omega(S) - \log\omega(x_i)+\Oh(1)$ for each $i\in[n]$;
    % \item $\rho(x_i)$ is lexicographically less than $\rho(x_j)$ for each $1\le i < j\le n$.
    \item\label{sfec_size} $|\overleftarrow{\pi}(x_i)|+|\overrightarrow{\pi}(x_i)|\in O(\log\log n)$ for each $i\in[n]$;
    \item\label{sfec_prev} $F(\rho(x_i),\overleftarrow{\pi}(x_i))=\rho(x_{i-1})$ for each $i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}$; and    
    \item\label{sfec_next} $F(\rho(x_i),\overrightarrow{\pi}(x_i))=\rho(x_{i+1})$ for each $i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}$.    
  \end{enumerate}
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
  Let $\omega':S\to\N^+$ be the nice weight function obtained by applying \cref{obs:nice} to $\omega$.  Then $\log\omega'(S)\le\log n+ O(1)$ and $\log\omega'(x_i)\ge 0$ for each $i\in[n]$.
  The prefix-free code $\rho$ is obtained by applying \cref{shannon_fano_elias_tree} to $S$ with weight function $\omega'$ to obtain a tree $T$ and then encoding each root-to-leaf path in $T$ with a bitstring as described above.  This gives a code $\rho$ that satisfies \ref{sfec_len}.
  
  Fix $i\in[n]$.  Let $y_0,y_1,\ldots,y_\ell$ be the path from $y_0:=x_i$ to $y_\ell:=x_{i-1}$ in $T$.  Let $j$ be such that $y_j=\lca_T(x_i,x_{i-1})$.  Then $y_{j+1}$ is the left child of $y_j$ and $y_k$ is the right child of $y_{k-1}$ for each $k\in\{j+2,\ldots,\ell\}$.  Thus, $\rho(x_{i-1})$ is the prefix of $\rho(x_i)$ of length $\depth_T(y_j)$, followed by a $0$ bit, followed by a string of $1$ bits of length $\ell-j-1$.  We set $\overleftarrow{\pi}(x_i):=\langle 0, \bin(j), \bin(\ell-j-1)\rangle$.  Since $j <\ell$ and $\ell\le 2\height(T)\in O(\log n)$, $\overleftarrow{\pi}(x_i)$ has length $O(\log\log n)$.  The code $\overrightarrow{\pi}(x_i)$ is defined similarly using the path from $y_0:=x_i$ to $y_\ell:=x_{i+1}$ in $T$.  Then $\overrightarrow{\pi}(x_i):=\langle 1, \bin(j), \bin(\ell-j-1)\rangle$.  

  The operation of $F$ is now obvious.  Given the arguments $\rho(x_i)$ and $\langle a,\bin(b),\bin(c)\rangle$, $F$ checks the value of $a\in\{0,1\}$.  If $a=0$, then it removes the suffix of length $b$ from $\rho(x_i)$ and appends the string $01^c$ to obtain $\rho(x_{i-1})$.  If $a=1$, then $F$ removes the suffix of length $b$ from $\rho(x_i)$ and appends the string $10^c$ to obtain $\rho(x_{i+1})$. 
\end{proof}

\subsection{On Multipart Labels}
\label{multipart}

In many cases, a label (a bitstring) consists of a sequence of parts (also bitstrings) of varying lengths, $s_1,\ldots,s_p$, where each $s_i$ may be empty.  The naive concatenation of $s_1,\ldots,s_p$ has length
\[
  \textstyle \sum_{i=1}^p|s_i| \enspace .
\]
This ignores the important issue that, in order for a decoder to extract the individual parts $s_1,\ldots,s_p$, it must know the number of parts $p$ and (at least $p-1$ of) the lengths $|s_1|,\ldots,|s_p|$.  We handle this by encoding the value of $p$ and the lengths $|s_1|,\ldots,|s_p|$ and prepending these to the concatenation. This is most easily done using Elias' $\gamma$-code $\gamma:\N^+\to\{0,1\}^*$ for positive integers, in which each $x\in\N^+$ is encoded by a self-delimiting binary string $\gamma(x)$ of length $|\gamma(x)|= 2\lfloor \log x  \rfloor  + 1$ \cite{elias:universal}.\footnote{For each $x\in\N^+$, $\gamma(x)$ consists of $\lfloor\log x\rfloor$ 0 bits, followed by the binary representation of $x$ (which begins with a $1$ bit and has length $\lfloor\log x\rfloor+1$).} In our application, each part $s_i$ may have zero length and $p$ may even be equal to zero, so we encode each $x\in\N$ using $\gamma(x+1)$, which has length $2\floor{\log (x+1)}+1$.
In this way, we obtain a self-delimiting encoding of $s_1,\ldots,s_p$ as
\[  \mathdefin{\langle s_1,\ldots,s_p\rangle}:=\gamma(p+1)\circ\gamma(|s_1|+1)\circ\cdots\circ\gamma(|s_p|+1)\circ s_1\circ\cdots\circ s_p \enspace ,
\]
where $\circ$ denotes string concatenation (and $\langle\rangle:=\gamma(1)$ when $p=0$). This encoding has length
\begin{equation}
  |\langle s_1,\ldots,s_p\rangle| = \textstyle\sum_{i=1}^p|s_i|+\Oh(\lgg p) + \Oh(\sum_{i=1}^p\lgg|s_i|) \enspace , \label{encoding_cost}
\end{equation}
where $\mathdefin{\lgg x} := \log\max\{2,x\}$ (so that $\lgg x$ is defined and $\lgg x\ge 1$ for each $x\in\N$).  The bits of $\langle s_1,\ldots,s_p\rangle$ that come from $s_1,\ldots,s_p$ are called the \defin{payload} bits and the remaining bits are called the \defin{bookkeeping} bits of $\langle s_1,\ldots,s_p\rangle$.
In our setting it will always be the case that $p\in o(\log n)$ and we will use \cref{obs:nice} to ensure that $|s_i|\in \Oh(\log n)$ for each $i\in[p]$.
Thus, the number of bookkeeping bits in $\langle s_1,\ldots,s_p\rangle$ is $\Oh(1 + p\log\log n)$.

In some cases, we include one or more non-negative integers as parts of a multipart label.  When we include $d\in\N$ as part of a label, we include its $\ceil{\log(d+1)}$-bit binary representation \defin{$\bin(d)$} (so $\bin(0)=\varepsilon$).



% \subsection{Separators with Low Pathwidth and Low Path-Adhesion-Width}\label{lowpw}

% Recall that for $b\in\R^+$, a rooted tree $T$ is $b$-skinny if $|L_i(T)|\le b$ for each $i\in\N^+$, and a rooted tree-decomposition $(T,(B_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ of a graph $G$ is $b$-skinny if $T$ is $b$-skinny.  The following lemma shows how to construct a $(1/b)$-separator using the bags in a $b$-skinny subtree of a tree-decomposition.

% \begin{lem}\label{low_pathwidth_separator}

\subsection{Working with Tree Decompositions}
\label{working_with_tree_decomps}

% In this section we the machinery needed to obtain an adjacency labelling scheme for any graph class $\calG'$
% This appendix provides an alternative proof of \cref{main_result} that can be used to construct an induced-universal graph with $n^{1+o(1)}$ vertices and edges. Eventually, this will replace the use of \cref{skinny_labelling,small_height}.  This also eliminates the need for \cref{disjoint_union_ii,b_skinny_decomp,skinny_edges,low_pathwidth_separator}.





In this section, we develop some tools for manipulating tree-decompositions like those obtained from \cref{gmpst}.


\begin{lem}\label{skinny_edges}
  Let $b>1$, let $T$ be a rooted tree, and let $\omega:V(T)\to\N^+$ be a weight function.  Then there exists $Y\subseteq E(T)$ such that
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=\rm(\alph*)] 
    \item each root-to-leaf path in $T$ contains at most $\log_b \omega(T)$ edges in $Y$; and
    \item $\omega(T_y)\le b\cdot\omega(T_x)$ for any two vertices $x,y$ in the same component of $T-Y$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
  The proof is by induction on $|V(T)|$.  Let $X_0:=\{x\in V(T):\omega(T_x)\ge \omega(T)/b\}$.  Since the root $r$ of $T$ satisfies $\omega(T_r)=\omega(T)>\omega(T)/b$ (as $b>1$) and every ancestor of a vertex in $X_0$ is also in $X_0$, the induced subgraph $T_0:=T[X_0]$ is a connected subtree of $T$ containing $r$.  If $X_0=V(T)$, then setting $Y:=\emptyset$ satisfies the lemma: each root-to-leaf path contains $0 \le \log_b\omega(T)$ edges of $Y$ (since $\omega(T)\ge 1$), and for any $x,y\in V(T)$, $\omega(T_y)\le \omega(T)\le b\cdot\omega(T_x)$ by the definition of $X_0$.

  Otherwise, let $F_0:=T-X_0$ and let $T_1,\ldots,T_s$ be the connected components of $F_0$.  By the definition of $X_0$, for any $x,y\in V(T_0)$ we have $\omega(T_y)\le\omega(T)\le b\cdot\omega(T_x)$.  Furthermore, for each $i\in[s]$, the component $T_i$ is the subtree $T_{y_i}$ rooted at the child $y_i\notin X_0$ of some vertex $x_i\in V(T_0)$, so $\omega(T_i)<\omega(T)/b$.  Since $r\in X_0$, each component $T_i$ satisfies $|V(T_i)|<|V(T)|$.  Applying induction to each $T_i$ with weight function $\omega|_{V(T_i)}$ yields an edge set $Y_i\subseteq E(T_i)$.

  Let $Y_0:=\{x_iy_i\mid i\in[s]\}$ and let $Y:=Y_0\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^s Y_i$.  Each component $C$ of $T-Y$ is either $T_0$ or a component of $T_i-Y_i$ for some $i\in[s]$.  In the former case, $\omega(T_y)\le b\cdot\omega(T_x)$ for all $x,y\in V(T_0)$ as established above.  In the latter case, since $(T_i)_z = T_z$ for every $z\in V(T_i)$, the inductive hypothesis implies $\omega(T_y)\le b\cdot\omega(T_x)$ for all $x,y\in V(C)$.  This establishes condition~(b).

  Finally, let $P$ be a root-to-leaf path in $T$.  If $P$ is contained entirely in $T_0$, then $E(P)\cap Y = \emptyset$, so $|E(P)\cap Y| = 0 \le \log_b\omega(T)$.  Otherwise, $P$ leaves $T_0$ via a unique edge $x_iy_i\in Y_0$ into some component $T_i$, and the suffix $P_i := P\cap T_i$ is a root-to-leaf path in $T_i$.  By induction, $|E(P_i)\cap Y_i|\le\log_b\omega(T_i)$.  Therefore,
  \[  
    |E(P)\cap Y|
      = |E(P)\cap Y_0| + |E(P_i)\cap Y_i|
      \le 1+\log_b\omega(T_i)
      < 1+\log_b(\omega(T)/b)
      = \log_b\omega(T) \enspace . \qedhere 
  \]
\end{proof}


The following lemma is a standard weighted separator theorem for trees; we include its short proof for completeness.

\begin{lem}\label{bag_separator}
  Let $T$ be a tree and let $\omega:V(T)\to\R^+$ be a weight function.  Then, for any $b\in\N^+$, there exists $S\subseteq V(T)$ with $|S|\le b-1$ such that $\omega(C)\le \omega(T)/b$ for each component $C$ of $T-S$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
  The proof is by induction on $b$.  If $b=1$, then $S:=\emptyset$ satisfies the requirements of the lemma since $T$ is the only component of $T-S$.  Now assume $b\ge 2$. Root $T$ at an arbitrary vertex. The set $\{v\in V(T):\omega(T_v)\ge \omega(T)/b\}$ contains the root of $T$ and is therefore non-empty. Let $x$ be a vertex in this set of maximum $T$-depth. Then $\omega(T_x)\ge\omega(T)/b$, while $\omega(T_y)<\omega(T)/b$ for each child $y$ of $x$.

  The components of $T-x$ consist of the subtrees $T_y$ for each child $y$ of $x$ (each having weight $\omega(T_y)<\omega(T)/b$), and possibly the component $T_0 := T-V(T_x)$ containing the parent of $x$. If $\omega(T_0)\le \omega(T)/b$ (or if $T_0$ does not exist), then $S:=\{x\}$ satisfies the lemma since $|S|=1\le b-1$ and every component of $T-x$ has weight at most $\omega(T)/b$.

  Otherwise, $\omega(T_0)>\omega(T)/b$. Since $\omega(T_x)\ge\omega(T)/b$, we have $\omega(T_0)=\omega(T)-\omega(T_x)\le (1-1/b)\omega(T) = \frac{b-1}{b}\omega(T)$. Applying induction to the tree $T_0$ with weight function $\omega|_{V(T_0)}$ and parameter $b-1$ yields a set $S_0\subseteq V(T_0)$ of size at most $b-2$ such that each component $C$ of $T_0-S_0$ satisfies $\omega(C)\le \frac{\omega(T_0)}{b-1}\le \frac{\omega(T)}{b}$.

  We define $S:=S_0\cup\{x\}$, so $|S|\le (b-2)+1 = b-1$. Each component of $T-S$ is either a component of $T_0-S_0$ (which has weight at most $\omega(T)/b$ by the inductive hypothesis) or one of the subtrees $T_y$ for a child $y$ of $x$ (which has weight $\omega(T_y)<\omega(T)/b$). Thus, $S$ satisfies the requirements of the lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lem}\label{log_b_td}
  Let $G$ be an $n$-vertex graph and let $\calT$ be a tree-decomposition of $G$.  Then, for any integer $b\ge 2$, there exists a rooted tree-decomposition $\calQ:=(Q,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q)))$ of $G$ and $Y\subseteq E(Q)$ with the following properties:
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=\rm(\alph*)]
    \item\label{req:height_0} each root-to-leaf path in $Q$ contains at most $\log_b n$ edges of $Y$;
    \item\label{req:subtree_size_0} each connected component of $Q-Y$ has at most $4b-1$ vertices;
    \item\label{req:subtree_torso_apex_0} for each $x\in V(Q)$, at least one of the following holds: 
    \begin{itemize}[nosep,nolistsep]
      \item\label{req:three_adhesions_0} $C_x$ is contained in the union of at most three adhesions of $\calT$; or
      \item\label{req:adhesion_torso_0} there exists an adhesion $A$ of $\calT$ such that $\torso{G}{\calQ,C_x}-A$ is an induced subgraph of some torso of $\calT$.
    \end{itemize}
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
  The proof is by induction, with a slightly more general inductive hypothesis. An inductive instance $(G,\calT, \alpha, \beta)$ involves a graph $G$, a tree-decomposition $\calT:=(T,(B_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ of $G$, and two (not necessarily distinct) leaves $\alpha,\beta$ of $T$ such that $|V(G)\setminus (B_\alpha\cup B_\beta)|\ge 1$.  We will produce a rooted tree-decomposition $\calQ:=(Q,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q)))$ of $G$ and $Y\subseteq E(Q)$ that satisfies all the requirements of the lemma as well as the following conditions, the first of which is a strengthening of \ref{req:height_0}:
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=\rm(\alph*)]\setcounter{enumi}{3}
    \item\label{req:root_0} $\alpha\in V(Q)$ and $\alpha$ is the root of $Q$;
    \item\label{req:height_prime_0} each root-to-leaf path in $Q$ contains at most $\log_b(n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta|)$ edges of $Y$; and
    \item\label{req:adhesion_pairs_0} the root bag $C_\alpha$ of $\calQ$ contains $A_\alpha\cup A_\beta$, where $A_\alpha$ and $A_\beta$ are the adhesions of $\calT$ incident to $\alpha$ and $\beta$, respectively.
  \end{enumerate}
  The induction is on the quantity $n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta| = |V(G)\setminus (B_\alpha\cup B_\beta)|$.  To see that this suffices to establish the lemma for an $n$-vertex graph $G$, we can attach two new leaves $\alpha$ and $\beta$ to $T$ to obtain $T'$, set $B_\alpha:=B_\beta:=\emptyset$ (with incident adhesions $A_\alpha=A_\beta=\emptyset$), and apply the refined result to $(G,(T',(B_x\mid x\in V(T'))),\alpha,\beta)$ to obtain the desired rooted tree-decomposition $\calQ$ and edge set $Y$.
   
  We now proceed with the inductive proof.  Root $T$ at $\alpha$.  For each $x\in V(T)\setminus\{\alpha\}$, let $A_x:=B_x\cap B_{p(x)}$ be the parent adhesion of $x$ in $\calT$, and set $A_\alpha:=\emptyset$.  Define the weight function $\omega: V(T)\to\N$ by $\omega(x):=|B_x\setminus (A_x\cup B_\alpha\cup B_\beta)|$.  Since each vertex in $V(G)\setminus (B_\alpha\cup B_\beta)$ belongs to $B_x\setminus A_x$ for a unique node $x\in V(T)$, we have $\omega(T) = n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta|$.

  Apply \cref{bag_separator} to the tree $T-\{\alpha,\beta\}$ with weight function $\omega$ to obtain a set $S_0$ of at most $b-1$ nodes of $T$ such that $\omega(C)\le\omega(T)/b=(n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta|)/b$ for each component $C$ of $T-S_0$.  Let $T'$ be the minimum subtree of $T$ spanning $S_0\cup\{\alpha,\beta\}$.  Let $S_1$ be the set of nodes of $T'$ that have degree at least $3$ in $T'$.  Then $|S_1|\le |S_0|\le b-1$.  Let $S:=S_0\cup S_1\cup\{\alpha,\beta\}$, so $|S|\le 2b$.  Let $G_S:=G[\bigcup_{x\in S}B_x]$.  Let $C_1,\ldots,C_s$ be the components of $T-S$.  For each $i\in[s]$, let $N_i:=N_T(V(C_i))$.  Since every branching node of $T'$ belongs to $S_1\subseteq S$, no component $C_i$ can have three or more neighbors in $S$, so $|N_i|\in\{1,2\}$ for each $i\in[s]$.

  We construct a tree $Q_0$ as follows: $V(Q_0)$ contains all nodes of $S$.  If $|N_i|=2$, we add a node $v_i$ to $Q_0$ adjacent to both nodes in $N_i$.  (If $|N_i|=1$, we do not add a node $v_i$.)  For each edge $xy\in E(T)$ with $x,y\in S$, we add the edge $xy$ to $Q_0$.  Since each component $C_i$ with $|N_i|=2$ corresponds to a path in $T'$ between two nodes in $S$, $Q_0$ is a tree with $V(Q_0)\supseteq S$ and $|V(Q_0)|\le 2|S|-1\le 4b-1$.  We root $Q_0$ at $\alpha$ (which is in $S$).
  
  For each $x\in S$, set $C_x:=B_x$.  For each $i\in[s]$ with $|N_i|=2$, write $N_i=\{a_i,b_i\}$ and let $\{x_i\}=N_T(a_i)\cap V(C_i)$ and $\{y_i\}=N_T(b_i)\cap V(C_i)$; we set $C_{v_i}:=(B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i})\cup (B_{b_i}\cap B_{y_i})$.  To ensure property \ref{req:adhesion_pairs_0}, we add the vertices of the adhesion $A_\beta$ to $C_x$ for each $x$ on the path from $\beta$ to $\alpha$ in $Q_0$.  This completes the definition of the bags $C_x$ for all $x\in V(Q_0)$.  Then $\calQ_0:=(Q_0,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q_0)))$ is a tree-decomposition of $G_S$.

  For each $i\in[s]$, let $G_i:=G[\bigcup_{x\in V(C_i)}B_x]$.  If $\omega(C_i) = 0$, then every vertex of $G_i$ is contained in $\bigcup_{x\in N_i} B_x$: when $|N_i|=1$, $V(G_i)\subseteq B_{a_i}\subseteq C_{a_i}$; and when $|N_i|=2$, $V(G_i)\subseteq (B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i})\cup (B_{b_i}\cap B_{y_i})\subseteq C_{v_i}$.  In either case, all vertices and edges of $G_i$ are already covered by a single bag of $\calQ_0$, so no further action is needed for $C_i$.

  For each $i\in[s]$ with $\omega(C_i)\ge 1$, we construct a tree-decomposition of $G_i$ as follows:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item If $|N_i|=1$, say $N_i=\{a_i\}$, then there is a unique node $x_i\in V(C_i)$ adjacent to $a_i$ in $T$. Let $T_i$ be the tree obtained from $C_i$ by adding a new leaf $\alpha_i$ adjacent to $x_i$, and set $B_{\alpha_i}:=B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i}$.  Then $\calT_i:=(T_i, (B_x\mid x\in V(T_i)))$ is a tree-decomposition of $G_i$.  Apply induction to the instance $(G_i,\calT_i,\alpha_i,\alpha_i)$.
    \item If $|N_i|=2$, say $N_i=\{a_i,b_i\}$, then there are nodes $x_i,y_i\in V(C_i)$ (possibly $x_i=y_i$) with $a_i x_i, b_i y_i\in E(T)$. Let $T_i$ be the tree obtained from $C_i$ by adding new leaves $\alpha_i$ adjacent to $x_i$ and $\beta_i$ adjacent to $y_i$, and set $B_{\alpha_i}:=B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i}$ and $B_{\beta_i}:=B_{b_i}\cap B_{y_i}$.  Then $\calT_i:=(T_i, (B_x\mid x\in V(T_i)))$ is a tree-decomposition of $G_i$.  Apply induction to the instance $(G_i,\calT_i,\alpha_i,\beta_i)$.
  \end{itemize}
  In either case, induction yields a rooted tree-decomposition $\calQ_i:=(Q_i,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q_i)))$ of $G_i$ with root $\alpha_i$, and an edge set $Y_i\subseteq E(Q_i)$.  By \ref{req:adhesion_pairs_0}, the root bag $C_{\alpha_i}$ contains $A_{\alpha_i}\cup A_{\beta_i}$ (where $A_{\beta_i}=A_{\alpha_i}$ if $|N_i|=1$).  If $|N_i|=2$, let $e_i$ be the edge between the root $\alpha_i$ in $Q_i$ and $v_i$ in $Q_0$.  If $|N_i|=1$, let $e_i$ be the edge between $\alpha_i$ in $Q_i$ and $a_i$ in $Q_0$.

  We assemble the tree $Q$ by taking $V(Q):=V(Q_0)\cup \bigcup_{i: \omega(C_i)\ge 1} V(Q_i)$ and $E(Q):=E(Q_0)\cup \bigcup_{i: \omega(C_i)\ge 1} (E(Q_i) \cup \{e_i\})$, and define $Y := \bigcup_{i\in[s]: \omega(C_i)\ge 1} (Y_i \cup \{e_i\})$.  Since $V(G_S)\cap V(G_i)\subseteq A_{\alpha_i}\cup A_{\beta_i}\subseteq C_{\alpha_i}\cap C_{u_i}$ (where $u_i\in\{v_i,a_i\}$ is the endpoint of $e_i$ in $Q_0$), $\calQ:=(Q,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q)))$ is a valid rooted tree-decomposition of $G$ with root $\alpha$, satisfying \ref{req:root_0} and \ref{req:adhesion_pairs_0}.

  Because each edge $e_i$ belongs to $Y$, the connected components of $Q-Y$ are precisely $Q_0$ and the connected components of $Q_i-Y_i$ for each $i$ with $\omega(C_i)\ge 1$.  Since $|V(Q_0)|\le 4b-1$ and each component of $Q_i-Y_i$ has at most $4b-1$ vertices by induction, property \ref{req:subtree_size_0} holds.

  Any root-to-leaf path in $Q$ consists of a path in $Q_0$ followed by at most one edge $e_i$ and a root-to-leaf path in $Q_i$ for some $i$ with $\omega(C_i)\ge 1$.  Since $E(Q_0)\cap Y=\emptyset$, the number of edges of $Y$ on this path is at most:
  \[
    1 + \log_b(|V(G_i)| - |B_{\alpha_i}\cup B_{\beta_i}|) = 1 + \log_b\omega(C_i) \le 1 + \log_b((n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta|)/b) = \log_b(n-|B_\alpha\cup B_\beta|) \enspace .
  \]
  Thus, property \ref{req:height_prime_0} holds.

  Finally, we verify property \ref{req:subtree_torso_apex_0}.  For each $i\in[s]$ with $\omega(C_i)\ge 1$ and each $x\in V(Q_i)$, every torso and adhesion of $\calT_i$ is also a torso or adhesion of $\calT$, so \ref{req:subtree_torso_apex_0} holds by the inductive hypothesis.  For each $x\in V(Q_0\setminus S)$, $x=v_i$ for some $i\in[s]$ with $|N_i|=2$; its bag $C_{v_i}$ is $(B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i})\cup (B_{b_i}\cap B_{y_i})$, plus possibly $A_\beta$, which is the union of at most three adhesions of $\calT$, satisfying \ref{req:three_adhesions_0}.  For each $x\in S$, the adhesion $A_\beta$ was added to $C_x$ (if $x$ is on the path from $\beta$ to $\alpha$), so $C_x\setminus A_\beta \subseteq B_x$.  Every neighbor of $x$ in $Q$ is either in $Q_0$ (where the adhesion is an adhesion of $\calT$ incident to $B_x$) or connected via an edge $e_i = x\alpha_i$ for some $C_i$ with $|N_i|=1$ and $x=a_i$, in which case the adhesion $C_x\cap C_{\alpha_i} = B_{a_i}\cap B_{x_i}$ is also an adhesion of $\calT$ incident to $B_x$.  Therefore, every clique edge of $\torso{G}{\calQ,C_x}-A_\beta$ is present in $\torso{G}{\calT,B_x}$, so $\torso{G}{\calQ,C_x}-A_\beta$ is an induced subgraph of the torso $\torso{G}{\calT,B_x}$, satisfying \ref{req:adhesion_torso_0}.
  \pat{Reread this proof.}
\end{proof}



A rooted forest-decomposition $(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F))$ of a graph $G$ is \defin{tidy} if
\begin{enumerate*}[label=(\arabic*)]
  \item $B_x\neq\emptyset$ for each $x\in V(F)$;  
  \item $B_y\not\subseteq B_x$ for each $y\in V(F)$ whose $F$-parent is $x$; and
  \item $B_z \cap B_y \not\subseteq B_y\cap B_x$ for each $z\in V(F)$ whose $F$-parent is $y$ and whose $F$-grandparent is $x$.
\end{enumerate*}



% \begin{lem}\label{tidy_forest}
%   Let $G$ be a graph and let $\calF:=(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F)))$ be a forest-decomposition of $G$ with the property that each torso of $\calF$ is a graph in $\calG$. Then there exists a graph $G'$ with an isolated vertex $v_0$ such that $G'-v_0=G$, a tree $T$ with root $x_0$, and a tidy rooted tree-decomposition $\calT:=(T,(C_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ of $G'$ that has the following properties:
%   \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=(\rm\alph*)] 
%     \item\label{tidy_vertex_subset} $V(T)\subseteq V(F) \cup \{x_0\}$ and $C_{x_0}=\{v_0\}$; 
%     \item\label{tidy_path_subset} $V(P_{T(x)})\subseteq V(P_F(x)) \cup \{x_0\}$ for each $x\in V(T) \setminus \{x_0\}$; and 
%     \item\label{tidy_tree_decomposition} $C_x\subseteq B_x\cup\{v_0\}$ for each $x\in V(T)$.
%     \item\label{adhesion} for each edge $xy$ of $T$, $C_x\cap C_y=B_x\cap B_y$ (if $x,y\in V(F)$) or $C_x\cap C_y=\{v_0\}$ (if $x=x_0$ or $y=x_0$).
%   \end{enumerate}
% \end{lem}

% \begin{proof}
%   First, we make the forest-decomposition $\calF:=(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F)))$ tidy in three steps:
%   \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=(\arabic*)]
%     \item Remove every node $x$ of $F$ with $B_x=\emptyset$.
%     \item Remove every edge $xy$ of $F$ with $B_x\cap B_y=\emptyset$.
%     \item Repeatedly replace an edge $yz$ of $F$ with the edge $xz$ if $B_z\cap B_y=B_x\cap B_y$ and $x$ is the parent of $y$.
%   \end{enumerate} 
%   Let $\calF'=(F',(B'_x\mid x\in V(F')))$ be the resulting tidy forest-decomposition.  Note that each torso of $\calF'$ is a torso of $\calF$, so each torso of $\calF'$ is in $\calG$.  Create the tree $T$ by introducing a vertex $x_0$ and adding an edge between $x_0$ and $x'$ for each root $x'$ of $F'$.  Set $C_{x_0}:=\{v_0\}$. For each child $x'$ of $x_0$, set $C_{x'}:=B'_{x'}\cup\{v_0\}$.  For each other vertex $x$ in $T$, set $C_x:=B'_x$.  Then $\calT:=(T,(C_x\mid x\in V(T)))$ is a tidy rooted tree-decomposition of $G'$ that satisfies \ref{tidy_vertex_subset}, \ref{tidy_path_subset}, \ref{tidy_tree_decomposition}, and \ref{adhesion}.
% \end{proof}



\begin{lem}\label{all_singing_all_dancing}
  Let $G_0$ be an $n$-vertex graph  and let $\calT$ be a tree-decomposition of $G_0$.  Then, for any integer $b\ge 2$,  there exists a graph $G$ with an isolated vertex $v_0$ such that $G-v_0=G_0$, a tidy rooted tree-decomposition $\calQ:=(Q,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q)))$ of $G$ rooted at $x_0\in V(Q)$, and a subset of edges $Y\subseteq E(Q)$ with the following properties:
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=\rm(\alph*)]
    \item\label{req:root_bag} $C_{x_0}=\{v_0\}$;
    \item\label{req:height} each root-to-leaf path in $Q$ contains at most $2\log_b n + 1$ edges of $Y$;
    \item\label{req:subtree_size} each connected component of $Q-Y$ has at most $4b-1$ vertices;
    \item\label{req:same_size} for each component $C$ and each $x,y\in V(C)$, $|\bigcup_{z\in V(T_x)} C_z|\le b|\bigcup_{z\in V(T_y)} C_z|$;
    \item\label{req:subtree_torso_apex} for each $x\in V(Q)$, at least one of the following holds: 
    \begin{itemize}[nosep,nolistsep]
      \item\label{req:three_adhesions} $C_x\setminus\{v_0\}$ is contained in the union of at most three adhesions of $\calT$; or
      \item\label{req:adhesion_torso} there exists an adhesion $A$ of $\calT$ such that $\torso{G}{\calQ,C_x}-(A\cup\{v_0\})$ is an induced subgraph of some torso of $\calT$.
    \end{itemize}
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lem}


\begin{proof}
  Let $\calT_0:=(T_0,(B_x\mid x\in V(T_0)))$ and $Y_0\subseteq E(T_0)$ be the tree-decomposition of $G_0$ obtained by applying \cref{log_b_td} to the tree-decomposition $\calT$.  Thus, each torso of $\calT_0$ satisfies \ref{req:subtree_torso_apex}.    
  First, we make the tree-decomposition $\calT_0$ into a tidy rooted forest-decomposition in three steps:
  \begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=(\arabic*)]
    \item Remove every node $x$ of $T_0$ with $B_x=\emptyset$.
    \item Remove every edge $xy$ of $T_0$ with $B_x\cap B_y=\emptyset$.
    \item Repeatedly replace an edge $yz$ of $F$ with the edge $xz$ if $B_z\cap B_y=B_x\cap B_y$ and $x$ is the parent of $y$.  If the edge $yz$ is in $Y_0$, replace $yz$ with $xz$ in $Y_0$.
  \end{enumerate} 
  Let $\calF=(F,(B_x\mid x\in V(F)))$ be the resulting tidy forest-decomposition and let $Y_1$ be the resulting subset of $E(F)$.  (For each $x\in V(F)$, the bag $B_x$ is well-defined since $V(F)\subseteq V(T_0)$.) 
  Note that each torso of $\calF$ is a torso of $\calT_0$. This construction gives an injective mapping from each edge $xy\in Y_1$ to an edge $x'y\in Y_0$ with the following property: for each $x\in V(F)$, $P_{F}(x)$ contains $xy\in Y_1$ if and only if $P_{T_0}(x)$ contains $x'y$. It follows that, for any connected component $C$ of $F-Y_1$, $V(C)\subseteq V(C_0)$ for some component $C_0$ of $T_0-Y_0$.  Therefore $|V(C)|\le |V(C_0)|\le 4b-1$.

  Let $Q$ be the tree obtained from $F$ by introducing a vertex $x_0$ and adding an edge between $x_0$ and $x'$ for each root $x'$ of $F$. Let $Y_2:=Y_1\cup \{x_0x'\mid x'\text{ is a root of }F\}$. Then each component of $Q-Y_2$ has at most $4b-1$ nodes.
  
  Let $G$ be the graph obtained by adding an isolated vertex $v_0$ to $G_0$.  Define $C_{x_0}:=\{v_0\}$. For each child $x'$ of $x_0$, define $C_{x'}:=B_{x'}\cup\{v_0\}$.  For each other vertex $x$ in $Q$, define $C_x:=B_x$.  Then $\calQ:=(Q,(C_x\mid x\in V(Q)))$ is a tidy rooted tree-decomposition of $G$ that satisfies \ref{req:root_bag} and \ref{req:subtree_torso_apex}.

  Let $A_{x_0}:=\emptyset$ and, for each edge $x\in V(Q-x_0)$, let $A_x$ be the parent adhesion of $x$ in $\calQ$.  For each $x\in V(Q)$, define $\omega(x):=|B_x\setminus A_x|$.  Since $\calQ$ is tidy, $\omega(x)\ge 1$ for each $x\in V(Q)$, so $\omega:V(T)\to\N^+$ is a weight function.  Let $Y_3\subseteq E(Q)$ be the set of edges obtained by applying \cref{skinny_edges} to $Q$ with the weight function $\omega$.  Then, for each component $C$ of $Q-Y_3$ and each $x,y\in V(C)$, we have   
  \[
      \textstyle \left|\bigcup_{z\in V(T_x)}C_z\right|=\omega(T_x)\le b\cdot\omega(T_y)=b\left|\bigcup_{z\in V(T_y)}C_z\right| \enspace .
  \]
  Let $Y:=Y_2\cup Y_3$.  Then $\calQ$ and $Y$ satisfy \ref{req:subtree_size} and \ref{req:same_size}.  Each root-to-leaf path in $Q$ contains one edge incident to $x_0$, at most $\log_b n$ edges in $Y_2$ and at most $\log_b n$ edges in $Y_3$.  Thus, each root-to-leaf path in $Q$ contains at most $2\log_b n+1$ edges in $Y$, so $Q$ and $Y$ satisfy \ref{req:height}.
\end{proof}


\section{Weighted Mixed Labelling Schemes}
\label{weighted_mixed_section}
 
% This section defines weighted mixed labelling schemes and proves our main technical result, namely \cref{PlainRealMainTechnical}. 
Let $\calG$ be a class of graphs. We say that $\tau:=(G^+,G,\omega)$ is a \defin{$\calG$-admissible triple} if $G^+$ is a graph in $\calG$, $G$ is a spanning subgraph of $G^+$, and $\omega:V(G)\to\R^+$ is a weight function.  Let \defin{$\calT_\calG$} be the set of all $\calG$-admissible triples. For each each $n\in\N$, let $\mathdefin{\calT_{\calG,\le n}}:=\{(G^+,G,\omega)\in\calT_\calG:|V(G)|\le n\}$.

Let $g_i:\N\to\R^+$ for each $i\in[3]$.
The class  $\calG$ has a \defin{weighted $(g_1,g_2,g_3)$ mixed labelling scheme} of $\calG$ if there exists a pair $(A,I)$ with
\begin{itemize}[nosep,nolistsep]
  \item $A:(\{0,1\}^*)^2\to\{0,1\}$ (the \defin{adjacency tester}); and
  \item $I:(\{0,1\}^*)^3\to\{0,1\}$ (the \defin{identity tester})
\end{itemize}
such that for every $n\in\N^+$ and every $(G^+,G,\omega)\in\calT_{\calG,\le n}$, there exist functions
\begin{itemize}[nosep,nolistsep]
  \item $\mu: V(G)\cup \{K\mid \text{$K$ is a clique in $G^+$}\} \to \set{0,1}^*$ (the \defin{mixed labelling function}) and 
  \item $\kappa:\{(K, u)\mid \text{$K$ is a clique in $G^+$}, u\in K\}\to \set{0,1}^*$ (the \defin{local identifier function})
\end{itemize} 
such that:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep,nolistsep,label=(M\arabic*)]
  \item\label{mls:injective_on_cliques} for all pairs of distinct cliques $K$ and $L$ in $G^+$, $\mu(K)\neq\mu(L)$;
  \item\label{mls:adjacency_tester} for all $u,v\in V(G)$, $A(\mu(u),\mu(v))=1$ if and only if $uv\in E(G)$;
  \item\label{mls:identity_tester} for every clique $K$ in $G^+$, every $u\in K$, and every $v\in V(G)$, $I(\mu(K),\kappa(K, u),\mu(v))=1$ if and only if $u=v$;
  \item\label{mls:vertex_label_length} for every $v\in V(G)$, $|\mu(v)| \leq \log \omega(G) - \log\omega(v) + g_1(n)$;
  \item\label{mls:clique_label_length} for every clique $K$ in $G^+$, $|\mu(K)|\le \log \omega(G) - \log(\min_{v\in K}(\omega(v))) + g_3(n)$;
  \item\label{mls:local_identifier_length}  for every clique $K$ in $G^+$ and every $u\in K$, $|\kappa(K, u)| \leq g_2(n)$.
\end{enumerate}
Here is a little intuition about the last three conditions. The function $g_1(n)$ measures how much the length of a vertex label exceeds the ideal value $\log \omega(G) - \log\omega(v)$ that appears in Kraft's Inequality \cite{Kraft1949}. The function $g_2(n)$ is the length of the longest local identifier.
